Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what brings me in is... I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like at the warehouse, I’ll be counting inventory, and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and I’m off. Just gone from the count.

And then later I realize I put the wrong numbers in, which is embarrassing because I’ve done this work a long time. I mean, warehouses are always noisy, they’ve always been noisy, but lately every little thing pulls me away.

Then I look back and I’ve put in the wrong numbers, and that just— it bothers me because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself acting on impulse or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I mean, sometimes. Because it’s more than I should’ve spent, and I didn’t really think it through at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious, exactly. Yeah… I’d say it’s affecting things, not like my whole life is falling apart, but enough that I notice it.

Yeah… sometimes I do. It depends on the day. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I’ve got this big burst of “I can fix the whole house and the workplace before dinner,” you know? Like suddenly everything seems solvable if I just move fast enough.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.

At the warehouse, I’ll be counting inventory and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and suddenly my brain just follows that. Then I come back to the count and I’ve put the wrong numbers in, or I can’t remember where I was. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless—I’ve done this kind of work a long time.

And then I start thinking, well, maybe I need a better system, or a different clipboard, or—my husband says I have about six half-started “systems” at home too, which is not really the point, but… yes. I am making mistakes and missing details more than usual.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly noticeable at work, I think, because there’s just so much going on around me. Like at the warehouse, if a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm starts, or somebody moves a pallet near me, my attention just jumps over there. Then I go back to the count and I’ve lost my place, or I’ve already put in the wrong inventory numbers.

When I’m alone… I mean, I can still get sidetracked, but it’s the noise and movement around me that really pulls me off. That’s what brought me in, honestly—I used to be able to keep my mind on the task better than this.

Then I look back and I’ve put in the wrong numbers, and that just— it bothers me because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself acting on impulse or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah... Now he’s there during the day, and things just feel… different, like the flow of the house is off.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing at work and at home causing problems in your relationships with others, such as your husband or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is causing some problems. I think it’s better when it’s quiet, yeah. Like if I’m not at work and there aren’t machines or people moving around, I can usually hold onto what I’m doing a little more.

At home it’s more like, I’ll start doing one thing and then something small pulls me away, but the warehouse is the big one. \n\nLike I said, a beeper goes off, or that forklift alarm starts, or someone’s moving a pallet and suddenly I’m not on the inventory anymore. Then I look back and I’ve put in the wrong numbers, and that just— it bothers me because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself acting on impulse or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I mean, sometimes. Not like I’m trying to be reckless, but I’ll get it in my head that I’m going to make something—like a project, you know, something useful—and then I’m at a craft market buying paints and frames and little tools.\n\nAnd I tell myself, “Oh, I’ll use all this,” but then the credit card bill comes and I’m just... embarrassed. Because it’s more than I should’ve spent, and I didn’t really think it through at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious, exactly. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me not keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of... especially lately after work. Which is weird, because usually after a shift I’d just want to sit down and be done.\n\nBut I’ll get home and feel sort of buzzing, like sitting on the couch feels impossible. I’ll start reorganizing the kitchen cabinets or sorting through bins in the garage—like, suddenly the storage tubs have to be fixed right then. And then I’ll find old Christmas stuff or tools my husband put in the wrong place, and next thing I know I’m knee-deep in all of it instead of just relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve always been this way, no. I mean, I’ve always had my little ways of doing things, everybody does, but this restless kind of feeling… it’s more recent.\n\nMaybe about a month? Around when my husband stopped working outside the home. Our whole routine changed, and I guess I didn’t realize how much that routine was keeping things steady. Now he’s there during the day, and things just feel… different, like the flow of the house is off.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing at work and at home causing problems in your relationships with others, such as your husband or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is causing some problems. At work I made a couple inventory mistakes, and I had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor, which I hate doing because I’ve been there long enough to know better.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband and I have been snapping at each other more. It’s not like big dramatic fights every time, but enough that the house feels tense. Like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of just getting used to him being home now that he’s retired. I’ll have schedules and notes like I’m running some big operation, when really it’s just me trying to keep everything organized.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s affecting things, not like my whole life is falling apart, but enough that I notice it.\n\nAt work, I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes, and that really bothers me because I like being dependable. And then at home, my husband and I keep arguing—not huge blowups, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired.\n\nI keep thinking if I could just get the house rhythm back, or maybe make better lists… but then I’m making lists about the lists, you know? So yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are significantly interfering with your ability to carry out daily activities?\n\nPatient: Significantly… I don’t know, maybe not like I can’t do anything, but it is getting in the way.\n\nLike at work, I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made inventory errors, and that’s embarrassing because I usually know my job. I’m not just tapping my foot or pacing, exactly… I’ll suddenly decide we need to fix some safety thing, like labeling an area better or rearranging how something’s stored, and then I’m making a whole list about it.\n\nAnd I’ve been signing up for more art workshops too, which isn’t work, I know—sorry—but it’s like I keep adding projects. I’ll have schedules and notes like I’m running some big operation, when really it’s just me trying to keep everything organized.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s affecting things, not like my whole life is falling apart, but enough that I notice it.\n\nAt work, I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes, and that really bothers me because I like being dependable. And then at home, my husband and I keep arguing—not huge blowups, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired.\n\nI keep thinking if I could just get the house rhythm back, or maybe make better lists… but then I’m making lists about the lists, you know? So yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are significantly interfering with your ability to carry out daily activities?\n\nPatient: Significantly… I don’t know, maybe not like I can’t do anything, but it is getting in the way.\n\nLike at work, I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made inventory errors, and that’s embarrassing because I usually know my job. And then at home, my husband and I have been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired. Which should be a nice thing, you know, but it’s been harder than I expected.\n\nSo yes, it’s interfering, just maybe not completely stopping me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to calm down when you're upset or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. It depends on the day. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I’ve got this big burst of “I can fix the whole house and the workplace before dinner,” you know? Like suddenly everything seems solvable if I just move fast enough.\n\nBut then if my husband questions one of my plans—something small, like why I’m reorganizing a closet right then—I can snap at him before I even really think. And then afterward I’m like, why did I make that into a whole thing? So calming down can take me a bit, especially when I already feel wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or forgetting important details in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll be counting inventory and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and suddenly my brain just follows that. Then I come back to the count and I’ve put the wrong numbers in, or I can’t remember where I was. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless—I’ve done this kind of work a long time.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, well, maybe I need a better system, or a different clipboard, or—my husband says I have about six half-started “systems” at home too, which is not really the point, but… yes. I am making mistakes and missing details more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're alone, or is it mostly when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly noticeable at work, I think, because there’s just so much going on around me. Like at the warehouse, if a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm starts, or somebody moves a pallet near me, my attention just jumps over there. Then I go back to the count and I’ve lost my place, or I’ve already put in the wrong inventory numbers.\n\nWhen I’m alone… I mean, I can still get sidetracked, but it’s the noise and movement around me that really pulls me off. That’s what brought me in, honestly—I used to be able to keep my mind on the task better than this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're on vacation or in quieter environments?\n\nPatient: I think it’s better when it’s quiet, yeah. Like if I’m not at work and there aren’t machines or people moving around, I can usually hold onto what I’m doing a little more.\n\nBut honestly, what brought me in is the warehouse stuff. I’ll be counting, and then a beeper goes off or the forklift alarm starts, or someone moves a pallet near me, and my attention just—goes with it. Then I look back and I’ve lost the count or I’ve put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nOn vacation… I don’t know, we don’t do big vacations much, and when we do I’m usually thinking about whether we packed snacks or where the sunscreen went—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I’m not just tapping my foot or pacing, exactly… I’ll suddenly decide we need to fix some safety thing, like labeling an area better or rearranging how something’s stored, and then I’m making a whole list about it.\n\nAnd I’ve been signing up for more art workshops too, which isn’t work, I know—sorry—but it’s like I keep adding projects. I’ll have schedules and notes like I’m running some big operation, when really it’s just me trying to keep everything organized.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s affecting things, not like my whole life is falling apart, but enough that I notice it.\n\nAt work, I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes, and that really bothers me because I like being dependable. And then at home, my husband and I keep arguing—not huge blowups, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired.\n\nI keep thinking if I could just get the house rhythm back, or maybe make better lists… but then I’m making lists about the lists, you know? So yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are significantly interfering with your ability to carry out daily activities?\n\nPatient: Significantly… I don’t know, maybe not like I can’t do anything, but it is getting in the way.\n\nLike at work, I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made inventory errors, and that’s embarrassing because I usually know my job. And then at home, my husband and I have been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired. Which should be a nice thing, you know, but it’s been harder than I expected.\n\nSo yes, it’s interfering, just maybe not completely stopping me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to calm down when you're upset or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. It depends on the day. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I’ve got this big burst of “I can fix the whole house and the workplace before dinner,” you know? And then afterward I’m like, why did I make that into a whole thing? Like at the warehouse, if a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm starts, or somebody moves a pallet near me, my attention just jumps over there. Then I go back to the count and I’ve lost my place, or I’ve already put in the wrong inventory numbers.\n\nWhen I’m alone… I mean, I can still get sidetracked, but it’s the noise and movement around me that really pulls me off. That’s what brought me in, honestly—I used to be able to keep my mind on the task better than this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're on vacation or in quieter environments?\n\nPatient: I think it’s better when it’s quiet, yeah. Like if I’m not at work and there aren’t machines or people moving around, I can usually hold onto what I’m doing a little more.\n\nBut honestly, what brought me in is the warehouse stuff. I’ll be counting, and then a beeper goes off or the forklift alarm starts, or someone moves a pallet near me, and my attention just—goes with it. Then I look back and I’ve lost the count or I’ve put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nOn vacation… I don’t know, we don’t do big vacations much, and when we do I’m usually thinking about whether we packed snacks or where the sunscreen went—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. In quieter places it’s not as bad. It’s really the noise and movement that pulls me off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness even in very quiet environments with minimal distractions?\n\nPatient: Even when it’s quiet… maybe a little, but it’s not like at work. What really brought me in is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore when I’m at the warehouse.\n\nI’ll be counting inventory, and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet, and my brain just—goes with it. Then I look back and I’ve lost the count or I’ve put the wrong numbers in. That’s the part that scares me, because I used to be able to do this without thinking so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety even when you're in a quiet room with nothing going on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, even if it’s quiet, I can still feel kind of wound up. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess it is. I keep telling myself it’s just little mistakes, but then I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor after messing up inventory, and that feels awful. Like I should know how to do this by now.

And at home it’s… not peaceful. My husband just retired, so we should be settling into that, figuring out breakfast routines or whatever people do—he makes coffee too strong, by the way—but instead we’re arguing enough that it feels like we’re just walking around each other. So yes, it’s distressing.

Yeah, it’s not just at work. Even on days off, or after I’m home, I still feel like I’m… buzzing, I guess. Like I should be tired after a shift, and I used to just sit down on the couch, maybe watch something with my husband.

I’ll start reorganizing the kitchen cabinets or sorting through bins in the garage—like, suddenly the storage tubs have to be fixed right then. And then I’ll find old Christmas stuff or tools my husband put in the wrong place, and next thing I know I’m knee-deep in all of it instead of just relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve always been this way, no. Now he’s there during the day, and things just feel… different, like the flow of the house is off.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing at work and at home causing problems in your relationships with others, such as your husband or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is causing some problems. At work I made a couple inventory mistakes, and I had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor, which I hate doing because I’ve been there long enough to know better.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband and I have been snapping at each other more. I’ll have schedules and notes like I’m running some big operation, when really it’s just me trying to keep everything organized.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s affecting things, not like my whole life is falling apart, but enough that I notice it.\n\nAt work, I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes, and that really bothers me because I like being dependable. And then at home, my husband and I keep arguing—not huge blowups, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired.\n\nI keep thinking if I could just get the house rhythm back, or maybe make better lists… but then I’m making lists about the lists, you know? So yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are significantly interfering with your ability to carry out daily activities?\n\nPatient: Significantly… I don’t know, maybe not like I can’t do anything, but it is getting in the way.\n\nLike at work, I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made inventory errors, and that’s embarrassing because I usually know my job. And then at home, my husband and I have been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired. Which should be a nice thing, you know, but it’s been harder than I expected.\n\nSo yes, it’s interfering, just maybe not completely stopping me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to calm down when you're upset or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. It depends on the day. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I’ve got this big burst of “I can fix the whole house and the workplace before dinner,” you know? Like suddenly everything seems solvable if I just move fast enough.\n\nBut then if my husband questions one of my plans—something small, like why I’m reorganizing a closet right then—I can snap at him before I even really think. And then afterward I’m like, why did I make that into a whole thing? So calming down can take me a bit, especially when I already feel wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or forgetting important details in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll be counting inventory and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and suddenly my brain just follows that. Then I come back to the count and I’ve put the wrong numbers in, or I can’t remember where I was. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless—I’ve done this kind of work a long time.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, well, maybe I need a better system, or a different clipboard, or—my husband says I have about six half-started “systems” at home too, which is not really the point, but… yes. I am making mistakes and missing details more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're alone, or is it mostly when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly noticeable at work, I think, because there’s just so much going on around me. Like at the warehouse, if a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm starts, or somebody moves a pallet near me, my attention just jumps over there. Then I go back to the count and I’ve lost my place, or I’ve already put in the wrong inventory numbers.\n\nWhen I’m alone… I mean, I can still get sidetracked, but it’s the noise and movement around me that really pulls me off. That’s what brought me in, honestly—I used to be able to keep my mind on the task better than this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're on vacation or in quieter environments?\n\nPatient: I think it’s better when it’s quiet, yeah. Like if I’m not at work and there aren’t machines or people moving around, I can usually hold onto what I’m doing a little more.\n\nBut honestly, what brought me in is the warehouse stuff. And then at home, my husband and I have been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired. Which should be a nice thing, you know, but it’s been harder than I expected.\n\nSo yes, it’s interfering, just maybe not completely stopping me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to calm down when you're upset or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. It depends on the day. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I’ve got this big burst of “I can fix the whole house and the workplace before dinner,” you know? Like suddenly everything seems solvable if I just move fast enough.\n\nBut then if my husband questions one of my plans—something small, like why I’m reorganizing a closet right then—I can snap at him before I even really think. And then afterward I’m like, why did I make that into a whole thing? So calming down can take me a bit, especially when I already feel wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or forgetting important details in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll be counting inventory and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and suddenly my brain just follows that. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless—I’ve done this kind of work a long time.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, well, maybe I need a better system, or a different clipboard, or—my husband says I have about six half-started “systems” at home too, which is not really the point, but… yes. I am making mistakes and missing details more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're alone, or is it mostly when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly noticeable at work, I think, because there’s just so much going on around me. Like at the warehouse, I’ll be counting inventory, and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or someone moves a pallet nearby—and I’m off. Just gone from the count.\n\nAnd then later I realize I put the wrong numbers in, which is embarrassing because I’ve done this work a long time. I mean, warehouses are always noisy, they’ve always been noisy, but lately every little thing pulls me away.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing or getting distracted at home or in other situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so... I mean, it’s most obvious at work because that’s where I’m doing counts and there’s numbers to mess up. At home it’s more like, I’ll start doing one thing and then something small pulls me away, but the warehouse is the big one. \n\nLike I said, a beeper goes off, or that forklift alarm starts, or someone’s moving a pallet and suddenly I’m not on the inventory anymore. Then I look back and I’ve put in the wrong numbers, and that just— it bothers me because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself acting on impulse or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I mean, sometimes. Not like I’m trying to be reckless, but I’ll get it in my head that I’m going to make something—like a project, you know, something useful—and then I’m at a craft market buying paints and frames and little tools.\n\nAnd I tell myself, “Oh, I’ll use all this,” but then the credit card bill comes and I’m just... embarrassed. Because it’s more than I should’ve spent, and I didn’t really think it through at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious, exactly. Overwhelmed maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to say because a lot of daily life is just… noisy, you know? Like there’s always something going on.\n\nAt home I’ll feel like there are too many things to do, and then I start thinking about the laundry or dishes, and then I remember I meant to call someone, and then—well, it kind of turns into a pile. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me not keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of... especially lately after work. Which is weird, because usually after a shift I’d just want to sit down and be done.\n\nBut I’ll get home and feel sort of buzzing, like sitting on the couch feels impossible. I’ll start reorganizing the kitchen cabinets or sorting through bins in the garage—like, suddenly the storage tubs have to be fixed right then. And then I’ll find old Christmas stuff or tools my husband put in the wrong place, and next thing I know I’m knee-deep in all of it instead of just relaxing.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve always been this way, no. I mean, I’ve always had my little ways of doing things, everybody does, but this restless kind of feeling… it’s more recent.\n\nMaybe about a month? Around when my husband stopped working outside the home. Our whole routine changed, and I guess I didn’t realize how much that routine was keeping things steady. Now he’s there during the day, and things just feel… different, like the flow of the house is off.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing at work and at home causing problems in your relationships with others, such as your husband or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is causing some problems. At work I made a couple inventory mistakes, and I had to go back and double-check things with my supervisor, which I hate doing because I’ve been there long enough to know better.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband and I have been snapping at each other more. It’s not like big dramatic fights every time, but enough that the house feels tense. Like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of just getting used to him being home now that he’s retired. It’s been… not awful, but not easy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also feel restless or fidgety at work, or is it mainly at home?\n\nPatient: It’s at work too, yeah, though it comes out a little different there. I’m not just tapping my foot or pacing, exactly… I’ll suddenly decide we need to fix some safety thing, like labeling an area better or rearranging how something’s stored, and then I’m making a whole list about it.\n\nAnd I’ve been signing up for more art workshops too, which isn’t work, I know—sorry—but it’s like I keep adding projects. I’ll have schedules and notes like I’m running some big operation, when really it’s just me trying to keep everything organized.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s affecting things, not like my whole life is falling apart, but enough that I notice it.\n\nAt work, I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor after making inventory mistakes, and that really bothers me because I like being dependable. And then at home, my husband and I keep arguing—not huge blowups, but enough that it feels like we’re sort of walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired.\n\nI keep thinking if I could just get the house rhythm back, or maybe make better lists… but then I’m making lists about the lists, you know? So yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these difficulties are significantly interfering with your ability to carry out daily activities?\n\nPatient: Significantly… I don’t know, maybe not like I can’t do anything, but it is getting in the way.\n\nLike at work, I’ve had to double-check with my supervisor because I made inventory errors, and that’s embarrassing because I usually know my job. And then at home, my husband and I have been arguing enough that it feels like we’re walking around each other instead of just settling into him being retired. Which should be a nice thing, you know, but it’s been harder than I expected.\n\nSo yes, it’s interfering, just maybe not completely stopping me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to calm down when you're upset or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes I do. It depends on the day. Some days I’m almost too upbeat, like I’ve got this big burst of “I can fix the whole house and the workplace before dinner,” you know? Like suddenly everything seems solvable if I just move fast enough.\n\nBut then if my husband questions one of my plans—something small, like why I’m reorganizing a closet right then—I can snap at him before I even really think. And then afterward I’m like, why did I make that into a whole thing? So calming down can take me a bit, especially when I already feel wound up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or forgetting important details in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nAt the warehouse, I’ll be counting inventory and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet nearby, and suddenly my brain just follows that. Then I come back to the count and I’ve put the wrong numbers in, or I can’t remember where I was. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless—I’ve done this kind of work a long time.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, well, maybe I need a better system, or a different clipboard, or—my husband says I have about six half-started “systems” at home too, which is not really the point, but… yes. I am making mistakes and missing details more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're alone, or is it mostly when you're around other people?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly noticeable at work, I think, because there’s just so much going on around me. Like at the warehouse, if a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm starts, or somebody moves a pallet near me, my attention just jumps over there. Then I go back to the count and I’ve lost my place, or I’ve already put in the wrong inventory numbers.\n\nWhen I’m alone… I mean, I can still get sidetracked, but it’s the noise and movement around me that really pulls me off. That’s what brought me in, honestly—I used to be able to keep my mind on the task better than this.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're on vacation or in quieter environments?\n\nPatient: I think it’s better when it’s quiet, yeah. Like if I’m not at work and there aren’t machines or people moving around, I can usually hold onto what I’m doing a little more.\n\nBut honestly, what brought me in is the warehouse stuff. I’ll be counting, and then a beeper goes off or the forklift alarm starts, or someone moves a pallet near me, and my attention just—goes with it. Then I look back and I’ve lost the count or I’ve put the wrong numbers into inventory.\n\nOn vacation… I don’t know, we don’t do big vacations much, and when we do I’m usually thinking about whether we packed snacks or where the sunscreen went—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. In quieter places it’s not as bad. It’s really the noise and movement that pulls me off.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness even in very quiet environments with minimal distractions?\n\nPatient: Even when it’s quiet… maybe a little, but it’s not like at work. What really brought me in is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore when I’m at the warehouse.\n\nI’ll be counting inventory, and then a beeper goes off, or the forklift alarm, or somebody moves a pallet, and my brain just—goes with it. Then I look back and I’ve lost the count or I’ve put the wrong numbers in. That’s the part that scares me, because I used to be able to do this without thinking so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety even when you're in a quiet room with nothing going on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, even if it’s quiet, I can still feel kind of wound up. The weird thing is after work, I used to come home and just collapse on the couch, but lately I come home still buzzing.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m going to sit down, and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a cabinet or sorting bins out in the garage. My husband will be watching TV and I’m like, “I’m just going to fix this one thing,” and then it turns into me moving stuff around for an hour. Sitting still just feels… impossible sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these difficulties are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I keep telling myself it’s just little mistakes, but then I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor after messing up inventory, and that feels awful. Like I should know how to do this by now.\n\nAnd at home it’s… not peaceful. My husband just retired, so we should be settling into that, figuring out breakfast routines or whatever people do—he makes coffee too strong, by the way—but instead we’re arguing enough that it feels like we’re just walking around each other. So yes, it’s distressing.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness even on your days off or when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just at work. Even on days off, or after I’m home, I still feel like I’m… buzzing, I guess. Like I should be tired after a shift, and I used to just sit down on the couch, maybe watch something with my husband.\n\nBut now I’ll sit for two minutes and then I’m up reorganizing a cabinet, or I end up in the garage sorting bins. Which is funny because some of those bins have been sitting there since forever—old Christmas stuff, tools that aren’t even mine—but suddenly it feels like I have to do it right then. Sitting still just feels impossible sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or work performance as a result of these difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. At work I’ve had to go back and double-check with my supervisor because I made some inventory mistakes, which is embarrassing because I feel like I should have that down by now.\n\nAnd at home… well, my husband just retired, so it should be this nice adjustment period, you know, figuring out our days together. Instead we’ve been arguing more than usual, to the point where it feels like we’re kind of walking around each other in the house. It’s not awful-awful, but it’s definitely causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity have been present since childhood, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say since childhood. I mean, I was never the most organized kid, but this feels different. It’s really been more like… maybe about a month? Since my husband stopped working outside the home and our whole routine changed.\n\nHe’s around more now, which is good, I mean it should be good, but the house rhythm is all different. Like even where the coffee mugs go has somehow become a discussion. But yeah, the focus and restlessness stuff—this is later, not something I remember always having.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
